### 5 September 2018 to present: cessation of TPG roll-out and recent developments

#### 5.1 The limited progress of TPG roll-out

112 At the time of TPG’s roll-out announcement in April 2017, a two to three year roll-out of approximately 2,000 to 2,500 macro sites plus small cells was envisaged.

#### 5.2 TPG ceases roll-out and writes down network

115 On 25 January 2019, the TPG Board resolved that TPG should cease the roll-out of its mobile network in Australia.

116 On 29 January 2019, TPG announced that it was ceasing its mobile network roll-out, as the Security Guidance had blocked its path to upgrade its mobile network to 5G. TPG stated that it did not make commercial sense to invest in further rolling out a network that, due to Huawei being the primary equipment vendor, could not be upgraded to 5G.

117 On 26 February 2019, TPG announced a write down of $228 million on its mobile spectrum and network assets.

118 On 29 March 2019, Mr Hanly presented to the TPG Board on the results of his investigations into alternative RAN vendors. The TPG Board decided that it was ‘clear that building a mobile network was not a feasible option’.

119 In April 2019, Mr Hanly attended further workshops and meetings with Nokia.

120 On 5 July 2019, Mr Ye, who reports to Mr Hanly, requested a meeting with Ericsson ‘to discuss more detailed solutions’, noting that TPG had ‘been solutioning via email about Ericsson solution for our poles’.

121 On 10 July 2019, Mr Ye requested a meeting with Nokia ‘to go through: (a). latest Nokia RAN hardware and feature roadmap, (b). Potential solutions for TPG scenario’.

122 On 10 July 2019, Mr Ye emailed Samsung to explain that TPG is ‘in the process of collecting 3.6GHz 5G radio product information for our internal evaluation, by any chance please can you help to fill the table below with updated information’.

123 On 5 September 2019, TPG released its FY19 results.

#### 5.5 Refresh of mobile plans

146 On 25 June 2019, Telstra launched a ‘refresh’ of its post-paid plans. This introduced major changes to the structure of Telstra’s mobile plans, with all plans now having ‘month to month’ no lock-in terms and ‘endless’ data, with speeds throttled to 1.5 Mbps at different data volumes depending on the price point of the plan.

147 In July 2019, Vodafone launched its own refresh of its post-paid plans. This included, in response to Telstra’s changes, the introduction of ‘endless’ data at a lower price point than previously available (on its $45 plan), with speeds throttled to 1.5 Mbps once customers reach the 30 GB data allowance. In September 2019, Telstra introduced four data SIM plans starting at $15 per month for 5 GB. All of these plans include ‘endless’ data, with speeds throttled to 1.5 Mbps once customers reach their data allowance, and free access to Telstra’s 5G network until 30 June 2020. Calls and SMS are not included in the plan, but can be made at extra cost.

A glossary of terms used in this Agreed Technology Primer (the Primer) is provided in Appendix A.

The Primer is intended to be a general description of mobile network technology and how it is used. For example, this document does not deal with the specifics of TPG's network plan or the proposed utilisation by TPG of small cells.

The parties to these proceedings agree that the facts in the Primer are "agreed facts" as defined in and for the purposes of section 191 of the Evidence Act 1995 (Cth) (the Evidence Act). In accordance with section 191(2) of the Evidence Act, the parties agree that:

 evidence is not required to prove the existence of any fact agreed in the Primer; and

 a party may not adduce evidence to qualify or contradict any facts agreed in the Primer.

### A MOBILE NETWORK DESIGN AND TECHNOLOGIES

1 A mobile network delivers mobile service such as voice calling, data transmission and messaging to mobile devices.

2 Wireless handsets or smartphones currently make up the majority of mobile devices, however future technology will see an increasing range of device types such as sensors and meters also connected to wireless networks.

#### A.1 Spectrum

##### A.1.1 Spectrum is an essential input in the supply of mobile services

3 Electromagnetic spectrum refers to the range of frequencies used to emit electromagnetic radiation. The electromagnetic spectrum is a continuous range of frequencies extending from the longest radio waves through to infra-red, visible light, ultra-violet, X-rays and gamma-rays.

4 Not all electromagnetic spectrum is used for radiocommunications. The radiofrequency spectrum (RF spectrum or simply spectrum) is that part of the total electromagnetic spectrum which is used for transmitting radio waves. Appendix B is a chart showing how the radiofrequency spectrum is used in Australia. As Appendix B shows, not all radiofrequency spectrum is used for mobile telecommunication services.

5 Spectrum is an essential input in the supply of mobile telecommunication services. Voice and data communications between mobile devices and a Radio Access Network (RAN) cannot occur without the use of appropriate and compatible spectrum.

6 The range of spectrum used for mobile telecommunication, commercial radio and other broadcast services (all forms of commercial radiocommunications) is generally limited to Very High Frequency (VHF) (30-300 MHz) and Ultra High Frequency (UHF) (300 MHz to 3 GHz) as this is currently most suitable, though frequencies between 6 GHz and 300 GHz are also useable for this purpose. Frequencies above 300 GHz are not used for commercial radiocommunications.

7 The frequencies used for mobile telecommunications in Australia are generally divided into three broad categories:

a) low band (between 700 MHz to 1 GHz);

b) mid band (1 GHz to 6 GHz); and

c) high band (above 6 GHz).

8 There are significant differences in the attributes of spectrum of different frequencies. Given these differences, an MNO will typically seek to have a range of spectrum to take advantage of the different attributes of the various frequencies as described in paragraphs 9 and 10 below.

9 For example, low band spectrum has greater propagation characteristics than mid band and high band spectrum — that is, the signal strength of radio transmissions at low band frequencies incurs less loss as it travels through a medium and is therefore maintained over longer distances, and penetrates better through obstacles such as trees. This means it provides superior coverage over broader geographic areas and superior in-building coverage. For example, 700 MHz spectrum propagates significantly further than 2.5 GHz spectrum. Similarly, millimetre wave spectrum (for example, 26 GHz) has not been used for generations of mobile technology prior to 5G due to its relatively poor propagation characteristics.

10 There is a finite amount of all spectrum, with the range of frequencies of low band spectrum being the most limited compared to mid and high band spectrum. The mid and high spectrum bands provide less coverage (including in-building coverage) than low band spectrum. As a wider range of frequencies (bandwidth) in the mid and high frequency spectrum bands are available, mid and high frequency spectrum bands provide greater capacity (the ability to carry more data) relative to low band spectrum.

##### A.1.2 The use of spectrum is regulated

11 Generally speaking, radio receivers (such as RAN equipment and mobile devices) are not able to distinguish between multiple simultaneous transmissions on the same frequency in the same geographic area. It is generally not desirable for example, for different mobile operators, or mobile operators and radio or TV broadcasters, to attempt to use the same spectrum bands to simultaneously transmit signals. This would cause interference which manifests as degraded quality and would result in failed communications (e.g., dropped calls or delays in transmission of data).

12 The use of spectrum is partitioned and highly regulated, locally and internationally, both to prevent interference between different users and different uses of spectrum, and to promote harmonisation between Australians’ use of spectrum and the use of spectrum in other countries.

13 Internationally, the International Telecommunication Union (ITU) coordinates a global standardisation process through which specific spectrum bands are designated for different services (television, satellite, military, mobile, etc.). Telecommunications equipment providers then develop their equipment and technologies in accordance with the ITU’s designation to ensure that they operate using standardised spectrum bands and spectrum configurations.

14 At the national level, there is a further level of regulation where the relevant government authority in each country designates what parts of the spectrum allocated for mobile use by the ITU will be used in that country by particular MNOs and on what terms.

15 As a result of this international and national regulation of spectrum, it is not possible for an MNO in Australia to decide to use any spectrum band it wishes. Spectrum needs to be designated internationally and allocated nationally for a specific use (e.g. mobile telecommunications), and global manufacturers need to manufacture and offer equipment and devices which are compatible with those specific spectrum bands.

##### A.1.3 Spectrum regulation in Australia

16 In Australia, the allocation of particular parts of the spectrum to particular users for particular uses is regulated by the Radiocommunications Act 1992 (Cth) (Radiocommunications Act), which provides for:[^1]

a) the preparation of a spectrum plan by the Australian Communications and Media Authority (the ACMA), which must:

i) divide into such number of frequency bands as the ACMA thinks appropriate so much of the spectrum as the ACMA thinks necessary for the purpose of regulating radiocommunications; and

ii) specify the purpose(s) for which each spectrum band may be used;

b) the designation by the Minister, after consultation with the ACMA, of a specified part of the spectrum to be allocated by issuing spectrum licences;

c) the preparation by the ACMA of marketing plans for issuing spectrum licences following a ministerial designation; and

d) the licensing of spectrum licensees to use particular parts of the spectrum as specified in each spectrum licence, as well as provisions making it unlawful to use a radiocommunications device otherwise than as authorised by a spectrum licence (or an apparatus licence or class licence) issued under the Radiocommunications Act.

17 To allocate spectrum licences for mobile telecommunications, the ACMA has generally conducted auctions for rights to use certain bands of spectrum in certain geographic areas.

18 To operate a mobile network in Australia, an MNO must acquire rights to use licensed spectrum for the geographic areas in which the mobile network is intended to operate.

19 The low bands used in Australia currently fall in the 700 MHz, 850 MHz and 900 MHz ranges and can be used for 3G, 4G and later 5G services.[^2] These bands have better coverage characteristics (including in-building coverage) and usually form the foundational coverage layer of mobile networks but are not suitable for carrying large amounts of traffic (relative to mid-band and high-band spectrum) as they are not allocated with a wide range of frequencies (bandwidth) for mobile telecommunications.

20 The mid bands used in Australia currently fall in the 1800 MHz, 2100 MHz, 2300 MHz and 2600 MHz ranges and can be used for 3G, 4G and later 5G services (when relevant 5G equipment and handsets supporting these bands are available). These bands have a shorter coverage range and less ability to provide coverage into buildings than low bands. However they are allocated with a wider range of frequencies (bandwidth), which makes them suitable for carrying larger amounts of traffic in dense areas.

21 Spectrum in the 3.4 GHz to 3.7 GHz frequency band (also mid-band spectrum) has been made available in accordance with the Radiocommunications Act including for use for 5G mobile services, and the 3.6 GHz band is the original band for 5G mobile services in Australia.

22 High Band Millimetre Wave (mmWave) spectrum is the set of spectrum bands emerging for use in mobile networks. In Australia, this will initially be in the 26 GHz frequency range. The characteristic of this frequency range is that it delivers a very short range, mainly line of sight, coverage.

23 Most standards from 1G to 4G[^3] have used the Frequency Division Duplex (FDD) configuration where ranges of frequencies are allocated in each of two directions (like a two way road), one range for transmission from the base station to the mobile device (downlink) and a second range of frequencies (the duplex) for transmissions from the mobile device to the base station (uplink). Separating these different directions of transmission into different frequencies prevents interference and allows simultaneous transmission to/from the base station.

24 More recent 4G and now 5G standards use an alternate frequency and transmission configuration known as Time Division Duplex (TDD) where the same frequencies are used to both send and receive between the mobile device and the base station. In order to avoid interference between the transmissions, the transmissions are divided in time (like sharing directions on a one-way road). In this configuration, when there is more data being transmitted from the base station to the mobile device than in the reverse direction, more of the time is allocated to downlink transmissions.

The objectives of Mobile JV Pty Limited are to optimise the acquisition, ownership, and supply (such as by sub-licensing) of licences to use parts of the spectrum by Mobile JV Pty Limited.

30 The Mobile JVA provides that in the absence of any approved plan by the Mobile JV Pty Limited Board, the parties to the Mobile JVA must allocate and sub-license spectrum licences acquired by Mobile JV Pty Limited to each shareholder or its nominee according to certain principles, including that, absent some other agreement, in areas where the two shareholder groups have not formed a shared RAN, the spectrum licence for any geographic area will be sub-licensed to each shareholder (or shareholder nominee) on the basis that each shareholder group will have equal spectrum bandwidth.[^11] In other words, the default position in such circumstances is that Mobile JV Pty Limited will sub-license spectrum equally between the TPG and VHA groups.

b) 40 MHz regional lots in Central Queensland, Northern Queensland, Southern Queensland/Regional Northern NSW, Regional Victoria and Tasmania;

c) 45 MHz regional lots in Regional Western Australia;

d) 30 MHz regional lots in Regional Southern/Western NSW; and

e) 20 MHz regional lots in Regional South Australia.

34 The spectrum lots referred to in paragraph 33 currently comprise all of Mobile JV Pty Limited’s spectrum holdings.

35 The ACMA is not scheduled to auction further mobile spectrum until July to December 2020, at which time the ACMA has indicated that it may auction 26 GHz mmWave spectrum and/or 850/900 MHz spectrum.[^13] The 900 MHz spectrum that is proposed to be auctioned is currently apparatus licensed to Telstra, Optus and VHA.

#### A.2 Components of a mobile network

36 A mobile network uses spectrum to connect mobile devices and deliver mobile services (such as voice, SMS and mobile data) to those devices.

37 Mobile networks have three primary components:

a) a radio access network or RAN, consisting of cell towers (also known as base stations sites or cell sites), and associated electronics and antennae on those sites, that communicate with mobile devices over designated spectrum;

b) a “backhaul’ transmission network, commonly fibre or microwave, which connects the RAN sites to the core network and thereby transmits information between the RAN and the core; and

c) a core network, which manages voice, SMS and/or data traffic, connects and manages the different parts of the network and connects to other networks (including the internet).

38 Each of these components is explained in further detail below.

39 An overview of the typical arrangement of these three primary components in a 3G / 4G network is shown in the diagram below:

##### A.2.1 Radio access network

40 The RAN usually consists of a large number of base station sites. The base station sites are deployed at locations that enable the RAN to provide mobile network coverage across a geographic area.

41 A base station site uses spectrum to communicate with a user’s mobile device by transmitting and receiving signals to and from the device, and provides mobile network coverage to mobile devices within that proximate geographic area.

42 The geographic area served by a base station site depends on a number of factors including, the output power of the radio equipment on that site, the spectrum bands used, the height at which the antenna is deployed and the surrounding topography and environment such as the presence of trees, hills or buildings.

43 The following equipment is typically deployed at a base station site:

a) one or more antennas and radio units, which transmit and receive radio frequency signals to and from mobile devices in the spectrum bands that the MNO has acquired the right to use;

b) a baseband unit, which processes signals and communicates between the multiple attached radio units on a base station site and the broader network and core network; and

c) related supporting infrastructure such as cooling, power and battery backup, site supervision and backhaul transmission system.

44 A diagram showing the typical arrangement of the radio equipment at a base station site with two MNOs appears below:

Note: This is one example of a macro base station site configuration.

45 The equipment deployed at a base station site may be mounted on different structures, such as:

a) communications tower or pole, or water tower (a tower);

b) the sides of buildings or on its rooftop;

c) power pole, lighting pole, traffic pole or utility pole (a pole); or

d) other suitable structures;

(collectively referred to as, a structure).

46 As discussed further in paragraphs 55 to 57, Macro RAN sites are typically deployed on towers, the sides of buildings or rooftops. Small cell sites are typically deployed on poles.

Radio units

47 The radio unit(s) deployed at a base station site may comprise a “remote radio unit” (RRU), which is installed on the structure, or an in-cabinet unit which is installed in a cabinet near the base of the structure. In general terms, it is preferable to install the radio unit closer to the antenna to minimise the length of the connecting cable as this minimises signal loss. There are corresponding challenges, however, as this increases the size, weight and complexity of the equipment to be located on the base station site's structure.

48 Some newer 4G technologies and 5G mid-band and mmWave band technologies combine the radio unit and antenna elements into a single radio and antenna package. This is known as an active antenna unit (AAU). AAUs may use a large number of antenna units, typically 16 or more, which is known as Massive MIMO. It is generally not feasible for RRUs to have Massive MIMO capability. Massive MIMO is explained in paragraph 49 below.

49 MIMO means “Multiple Input Multiple Output” and refers to a radio transmission technique where multiple signals are sent and received from different antennae to create additional data bandwidth. In mobile networks, typically 2 to 4 antennae are used for MIMO transmission. Massive MIMO refers to MIMO transmission with a much larger number of antenna units, at this stage, typically available from vendors with between 16 and 128 antenna units to transmit signals and the same number of antenna units to receive signals.[^14] A Massive MIMO AAU can both take advantage of the benefits of sending a number of data streams to an individual or many receiving mobiles as per MIMO, as well as being able to direct the signal beam towards the target mobile(s) or to reduce interfering signals received from unwanted mobiles ("beam forming").

50 Generally, the more powerful a radio unit, the better its signal and the greater coverage and capacity it can provide. As the power of a radio unit increases, the size, weight and electricity requirements of the radio unit generally also increase, which may affect the places in which the radio unit can practically be deployed. The size of the antenna can also affect the coverage and capacity provided by a base station site.

Baseband units

51 In some 4G and evolving 5G network configurations baseband units can be centralised to co-ordinate a larger geographic area of multiple base stations. This places the baseband unit at a point of central control and co-ordination known as a baseband hotel, Central-RAN (C-RAN) or Cloud RAN configuration, rather than at each base station site. These configurations rely on optical fibre connection and/or high speed data lines to connect the RRUs from each cell to the C-RAN location. C-RANs are new emerging technology around the world. They can only practically be deployed where there is a dense arrangement of base station sites (i.e. a number of base station sites located in close proximity to the baseband hotel).

52 A base station site can only receive and transmit signals over certain compatible bands of RF spectrum. This is because the equipment in the base station site has been designed and configured to operate only on particular configurations of specific spectrum bands. If an MNO acquires new spectrum in a different band (or in some cases even in the same spectrum band), it may be required to replace components in its RAN or add additional RAN components in order for the network to communicate using the new spectrum frequencies.

53 The same is true when there is a generational change in the mobile technology used by a RAN (see section A.5 below for a description of different mobile technology generations). Such a generational change will usually necessitate some equipment in the base station sites being replaced or upgraded to support the next generation of mobile technology. Generational change in mobile technology may also require some mobile devices attaching to that network to be replaced. New mobile technology generations are generally backwardly compatible and therefore do not generally require the replacement or upgrade of all base station site equipment or mobile devices.

Macro RAN sites and small cell sites

54 In general terms, MNOs deploy two main types of outdoor base station sites in a mobile network: macro RAN sites and small cell sites. These are pictured below:

Small Cell Site

Macro RAN Site

55 Macro RAN sites provide coverage over a large geographic area. Macro RAN sites are typically located at a height of 15 to 30 metres above the ground and overlook a wide area, where the radio waves can propagate above trees and surrounding buildings and terrain, or other obstructions. Macro RAN sites typically carry heavier, larger and higher-powered equipment relative to small cell sites.

56 Macro RAN sites are typically located on the rooftop or sides of buildings, towers (as depicted in the macro RAN site image above in paragraph 54) and other elevated structures which are strong. A combination of the equipment on macro RAN sites and the cost associated with leasing and erecting the structure on macro RAN sites means that they are generally significantly more expensive to deploy than small cell sites (on a per site basis).

57 Small cell sites provide coverage over a smaller geographic area than macro RAN sites. Small cell sites are base stations located on poles, street furniture or other lower structures. Small cell sites are typically located at a height of 3 to 10 metres above the ground, which is below the height of surrounding infrastructure and buildings. This impacts the effectiveness of the propagation of radio waves achievable from small cell sites and therefore the coverage provided by small cell sites, as compared to macro RAN sites, although the actual coverage area will vary depending on factors such as the output power of the small cell site and the surrounding topography and environment. Small cell sites also typically carry lighter, smaller and less high-powered equipment than macro RAN sites, which lowers the capacity and coverage which can be provided through a small cell site as compared to a macro RAN site. Capacity and coverage in the context of a mobile network are explained in paragraphs 75 and 76 below.

58 Small cell sites are typically used by MNOs to complement macro RAN sites, to provide additional capacity or address deficient coverage in a specific location (e.g., inside buildings or densely populated areas like transport hubs), or at locations that could not support a macro RAN site.

59 The diagram below shows examples of small cell site configurations on utility poles:

60 Telstra, Optus and VHA’s existing mobile networks in Australia primarily comprise macro RAN sites.

61 No mobile network in Australia currently uses small cell sites as the principal means of delivering coverage across its RAN. All existing mobile networks in Australia use macro RAN sites for this purpose.

62 In addition to macro RAN sites and small cell sites, there are also in-building systems that are designed to serve signals inside buildings such as in some CBD areas (e.g. shopping centres, transport tunnels and underground carparks). These consist of distributed antenna systems cabled throughout the building or tunnel. These are referred to as in-building coverage systems. Due to their costs, in-building coverage systems are typically only deployed where the MNO is unable to provide sufficient in-building coverage through the closest macro RAN site.

63 A macro RAN site of one MNO can be co-located on a structure at an existing base station site of other MNOs. With co-located macro RAN sites, the structure is shared but each MNO installs its own antennas and other equipment.

##### A.2.2 Transmission network

64 In a mobile network, transmission networks provide a link between the RAN and the core network and transmit base station control signals as well as information (voice, SMS, and data traffic) between the two.

65 Transmission networks may utilise a range of technologies, including optical fibre, coaxial cable, copper cables, satellite or microwave links.

66 Transmission networks are also used to connect the core network with other networks such as other domestic and international mobile and fixed phone networks, and major Internet Points-of-Presence.

67 The services supplied over transmission networks are known as “transmission”, with “backhaul” services referring to transmission between a base station site and the core network. Transmission services enable large volumes of aggregated communications traffic to be carried from one point to another.

68 MNOs can self-supply transmission services if they have their own transmission network. Alternatively, MNOs can utilise third-party transmission infrastructure for this purpose. Where third-party infrastructure is used, an MNO can either acquire transmission services from the third party, or instead lease “dark fibre” (unused optical fibre cores in optical fibre cable that has been deployed by a third party) and use its own optical transmission equipment to provide transmission services over that dark fibre.

69 Transmission networks are typically designed in a ring topology which means that a number of base station sites are often connected to a common aggregation point before connection to the core. One of the key reasons for this is a requirement for redundancy. Each base station site is connected via at least two routes, making it possible to continue delivering transmission services to a base station site through one link when one of the other fibre links in the ring is severed. Transmission networks can also be designed in a hub and spoke topology.

70 There is also a backhaul architecture option available known as Cloud RAN or C-RAN (described above at paragraph 51). MNOs may choose to invest in this new architecture, depending on the MNO's current transmission network architecture and the cost of deploying a C-RAN.

71 The diagram below shows this new backhaul architecture option.

##### A.2.3 Core network

72 The core network manages voice, SMS and data traffic, connects and manages the different parts of the network and connects to other networks (including the internet).

73 The core network includes an intelligent central routing system which facilitates connection of different parts of the RAN as well as communication with other networks, including the internet. It is also responsible for functions such as device authentication, generating charging records and user management.

74 Without a core network, devices cannot be authenticated and connected to the network and users on one network cannot communicate with users on another network.

#### A.3 Capacity and Coverage

75 There are two key aspects of network design for a mobile network:

a) "coverage" - A mobile network’s "coverage" is the geographic locations that a customer's mobile device is able to connect to a base station site in the mobile network, alongside a number of other customers connected to the network in that geographic location.

b) "capacity" - A mobile network’s "capacity" refers to the amount of combined data bandwidth (the amount of data that can be transmitted in a period of time, usually measured in bits per second (bps)) a network has to support the volume of voice and data traffic generated by multiple users on a network at defined performance standards.

76 The capacity of a mobile network is affected by:

a) the amount of spectrum the MNO has rights to, including the number of bands, the amount of spectrum in each band, and the distribution across high, mid and low bands;

b) the location, number and type of macro RAN sites and small cell sites;

c) the capability and type of the equipment used in the mobile network, including spectral efficiency of the technology used; and

d) the capacity of the transmission network.

#### A.4 Roaming Agreements

77 A roaming agreement essentially allows an MNO to provide mobile services outside of its own network footprint. That service is provided at a charge to the MNO whose customers are ‘roaming’, usually calculated on the basis of each minute of use, message sent or megabyte of data used. Domestic roaming agreements are generally used in respect of the geographic edge of mobile networks. Mobile roaming agreements are agreements between MNOs. The existing roaming agreements in Australia allow retail mobile services customers of one MNO to use their mobile device on another MNO’s network when outside the coverage area for the mobile network for which they subscribe.

#### A.5 Evolution in mobile technologies

78 Mobile technology has developed over time. These developments are reflected in changes in the industry standards applicable to mobile technology. Such standards facilitate interoperability between mobile network equipment, mobile devices and the spectrum used to communicate between mobile networks and devices.

79 There are broadly five "generations" of mobile technology that have developed globally.

80 The first generation of mobile technology (1G) was developed in the 1970s and 1980s. 1G technology was based on analogue technology and the standards were developed both nationally and regionally. 1G mobile networks and devices could only be used for voice calls and did not support SMS messaging or data services. 1G did not permit international roaming and the different 1G technologies were not interoperable — for example, a North American Advanced Mobile Phone System (AMPS) handset could not connect to a British TACS network.

81 The second generation of mobile technology was developed in the early 1990s. In Europe, the European Telecommunications Standards Institute (ETSI) oversaw the development of the Global System for Mobile Communications (GSM), which is a standard for mobile devices now commonly referred to as "2G".

82 2G mobile technology was digital. While 2G mobile technology was primarily designed to support voice services, it also facilitated new features in mobile technology such as SMS and international roaming.

83 The GSM standard defined all inter-working technical aspects of mobile equipment, devices and spectrum bands which allowed different equipment to be used by different network providers and different handsets to be used with different networks. This helped to facilitate a harmonisation of mobile telecommunications across borders and allowed devices to roam on other networks. By the mid-1990s, GSM was used in a number of countries around the world, including by operators in the United States, Asia and Australia. The primary other 2G standard was CDMA (Code Division Multiple Access), which was primarily used in the USA and Russia, while other 2G standards such as Digital AMPS (D-AMPS) were used in North America, South America and Korea.

84 As 2G technology developed, it became capable of providing limited data services with data speeds of approximately 20 to 150 kilobits per second (Kbps), which was enough for slow web browsing (primarily for text and small images) and email.

85 In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the third generation of mobile technology (3G) was developed.

86 In Europe, the 3G Partnership Project (3GPP) was created under ETSI to develop a 3G standard which became known as the Universal Mobile Telecommunications System (UMTS). In the US, the Telecommunications Industry Association and American National Standards Institute developed a separate 3G standard known as CDMA2000. The International Telecommunication Union (ITU) created an overarching capabilities requirement known as "IMT-2000", the fulfilment of which enabled different technology standards to be deemed 3G. Both UMTS and CDMA2000 complied with IMT-2000, but they were not compatible so European UMTS devices and CDMA2000 devices could not connect on each other's network.

87 The development of 3G technology facilitated much greater data use by mobile devices. When 3G was first introduced, it offered data speeds of up to 384 Kbps. As 3G technology improved under the High Speed Packet Access (HSPA) standards, this increased to about 21 megabits per second (Mbps) (21,000 Kbps) for a single 5 MHz carrier and 42 Mbps peak speeds under Dual Carrier HSPA (DC-HSPA) using two 5 MHz carriers.

88 The fourth generation of mobile technology (4G) was developed in the mid-2000s and MNOs began offering services on 4G mobile networks from 2011.

89 The ITU created a capabilities requirement for 4G mobile technology known as "IMT Advanced". The first technical standard to achieve this was developed by 3GPP/ETSI and is known as "Long Term Evolution" (LTE). The LTE standard facilitated peak theoretical data speeds of up to 150 Mbps for a Category 4 device using 20 MHz of spectrum.

90 As with earlier technologies, 4G standards have continued to develop over time whereby new categories of devices with the aggregation of multiple combinations of 20 MHz LTE carriers can theoretically deliver peak speeds of over 1 Gbps.

91 The speeds in paragraphs 89 and 90 above are theoretical speeds and typical user speeds are usually much lower than peak network speeds due to multiple users sharing the bandwidth and the impact of varying radio conditions. Today, typical average speeds on a 4G network can range up to a few 10s of Mbps, depending on the spectrum available and the number of users.

92 4G LTE networks are backwardly compatible with 2G GSM and 3G UMTS networks. In Australia, 4G LTE networks were initially deployed alongside existing 2G and 3G networks, which continued to operate. Over time, Australian MNOs began shutting down their 2G networks to redeploy (or “refarm”) spectrum for use in their 3G and 4G networks.

93 5G is a collection of next generation mobile technologies following 4G. The target capabilities for 5G have been set out by the ITU under the "IMT-2020" designation. The technical standards to achieve these capabilities are currently being developed.

94 5G is typically described as providing for three broad capabilities:

a) Enhanced Mobile Broadband (eMBB), which involves delivering greater speed and capacity than is currently available using previous generations of mobile technologies. 5G is expected to deliver typical data download speeds of approximately 100 Mbps. 5G technology offers the prospect of peak data download speed of up to 20 Gigabits per second (Gbps) using high frequency spectrum bands.

b) Massive Machine Type Communications (MMTC), which involves enabling mobile networks to manage connectivity with a much larger number of connected devices than was previously possible. This will permit thousands or even millions of connections per base station site, thereby facilitating a much larger number of Internet-connected devices supported across each mobile network. This capability will support the evolution of the Internet of Things, being the connection of everyday objects, devices, tools and machines to the Internet;

c) Ultra-Reliable Low Latency Communications, which means that mission critical systems requiring very low latency and very high reliability such as automated factories and autonomous cars can communicate and be managed in near real-time.

95 The technical standards in the RAN for these three principal capabilities, and several others, have been completed by ETSI and 3GPP as part of 3GPP's Release 15. Standards for further capabilities both in the RAN and in the Core Network (5G Core) are expected in 2020 as part of 3GPP's Release 16.

#### A.6 5G mobile network technology vendors

96 At present, there are five principal vendors globally that design, manufacture and supply the telecommunications equipment necessary to deploy a 5G network: Huawei Technologies Co Ltd (Huawei), Ericsson, Nokia, ZTE and Samsung. Three of these vendors are currently active in Australia - Huawei, Ericsson and Nokia.

97 The 5G radio network equipment designed, manufactured and supplied by each of Huawei, Ericsson and Nokia in Australia is proprietary and not currently designed to be interoperable, and is not currently interoperable at the radio base station level, with the 5G technology of the other two vendors in each case when used on the same sites in the same mobile network.

98 The 5G radio network equipment designed, manufactured and supplied by each of Huawei, Ericsson and Nokia is also not interoperable with the 4G network equipment of the other two vendors in each case. For example, a 5G AAU of one vendor cannot interoperate with a 4G baseband unit of another vendor.

#### A.7 Mobile networks in Australia

99 There are currently three mobile networks in operation in Australia, owned by Telstra, Optus and VHA respectively.

100 Each of Telstra, Optus and VHA’s existing mobile networks uses 3G and 4G mobile technology. Telstra has announced that it has started rolling out a 5G mobile network with limited geographic coverage. Optus has announced plans for a 5G home broadband service, being a fixed wireless service.

101 Telstra launched its 4G network in 2011, Optus launched its 4G network in 2012 and VHA launched its 4G network in 2013.

102 In order to roll out a 5G mobile network, MNOs will need to deploy new 5G-specific equipment that is capable of using standardised 5G technologies to provide services to 5G-capable devices.

103 Conceivably, a 5G network could be implemented in a number of options as defined by the 3GPP. Two of these options primarily being considered for 5G evolution are:

a) a non-standalone or “NSA” network using option 3 defined by the 3GPP: in this type of 5G network, larger elements of an existing 4G network (including the 4G RAN, transmission and core), are required and used to provide a 5G service; and

b) a standalone or “SA” network using option 2 defined by the 3GPP: in this type of 5G network, there is no dependency on a 4G network to provide 5G services.

104 The difference between the two primary options currently being considered for NSA and SA networks is shown graphically below by way of an example of a design which can be used to deploy SA option 3 and NSA option 2 networks:

105 In the NSA 5G configuration, the 4G Core Network (Enhanced Packet Core) is upgraded for 5G. The 5G Radio is anchored onto a 4G Radio. The dotted lines represent the control signals for 4G and 5G. The solid lines represent the data traffic carried to/from the mobile device. This configuration is able to use the existing 4G core network to delivery additional data capacity over 5G spectrum bands.

106 In the 5G SA configuration, a new 5G Core Network connects only to 5G base stations for control and data transmissions. The evolution of the 5G core will be used to enable the evolution of 5G services beyond eMBB.

107 The 5G network proposed to be deployed by VHA is an NSA network. The 5G networks currently being deployed by Telstra and Optus are also NSA networks.

108 To deploy a 5G NSA network, an MNO would ordinarily use the same radio base station network equipment vendor as it used for its 4G radio base station network given that the 5G radio base station equipment supplied by Huawei, Ericsson or Nokia (as the case may be) is not compatible with the 4G radio base station network equipment supplied by the other vendors in each case. To use a different 5G radio base station network equipment vendor to the existing 4G network equipment vendor would necessarily involve increased cost and risk given the prospect of technology incompatibilities such as those discussed at paragraph 98 above.

109 There are presently no SA 5G networks in Australia.

110 There is presently no 5G Core equipment available for use in a 5G SA RAN configuration in Australia, and no SA 5G handsets available in Australia.

111

### APPENDIX A: GLOSSARY

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| 1G | The first generation of mobile technology developed in the 1970s and 1980s. |
| 2G | The second generation of mobile technology developed in the early 1990s. |
| 3G | The third generation of mobile technology developed in the late 1990s and early 2000s. |
| 3GPP | 3G Partnership Project. |
| 4G | The fourth generation of mobile technology developed in the mid-2000s. |
| 5G | The fifth generation of mobile technology. 5G is a collection of next generation mobile technologies following 4G. The capabilities for 5G have been set out by the ITU under the "IMT 2020", albeit that the technical standards to achieve some of these capabilities are still being developed. |
| AAU | Active Antenna Unit. 4G technologies and 5G mid-band and mmWave band technologies which combine the radio unit and antenna elements into a single radio and antenna package. |
| ACMA | Australian Communications and Media Authority. |
| AMPS | Advanced Mobile Phone System. A first generation analogue mobile phone system. |
| antenna | An antenna is a physical array of conductors, at specific lengths that transform specific frequency ranges of radio waves to/from electric signals. |
| backhaul services | Transmission between a base station site and the core network. |
| backhaul transmission network | A transmission network, commonly fibre or microwave, which connects the RAN sites to the core network and thereby transmits information between the RAN and the core. |
| base station sites | A base station site uses spectrum to communicate with a user’s mobile device by transmitting and receiving signals from and to the device, and provides mobile network coverage to mobile devices within that proximate geographic area. Macro RAN sites and small cell sites are two main types of outdoor base station sites. |
| baseband unit (BBU) | A unit that processes signals and communicates between the multiple attached radio units on a base station site and the broader network and core network. |
| bps | Bits per second |
| capacity | The amount of combined data bandwidth a network has to support the volume of voice and data traffic generated by multiple users on a network at defined performance standards. |
| CDMA | Code Division Multiple Access, a 2G standard used in the USA and Russia. |
| core network | Component of a mobile network which manages voice and/or data traffic, connects and manages the different parts of the network and connects to other networks (including the internet) |
| coverage | The geographic locations that a customer's mobile device is able to connect to a base station site in the mobile network, alongside a number of other customers connected to the network in that geographic location. |
| D-AMPS | Digital AMPS, a 2G standard used in North America, South America and Korea |
| dark fibre | Unused optical fibre cores in optical fibre cable that has been deployed by a third party without optical equipment (to communicate data signals on the fibre) at each end of the fibre. Dark fibre is subsequently “lit up” by a user with the user's own optical equipment, allowing the fibre to be used by that user. |
| data bandwidth | The amount of data transferred in a period of time (usually measures in bps). |
| electromagnetic spectrum | The range of frequencies used to emit electromagnetic radiation. The electromagnetic spectrum is a continuous range of electromagnetic radiation extending from the longest radio waves through to infra-red, visible light, ultra-violet and X-rays to gamma-rays. |
| ETSI | European Telecommunications Standards Institute. |
| Gbps | Gigabits per second |
| GSM | Global System for Mobile Communications, which is a standard for mobile devices now commonly referred to as "2G". |
| high band | Mobile network frequencies above 6 GHz. |
| in-cabinet unit | A radio unit that is installed in a cabinet near the base of the structure. |
| interference | Unintended radio signals being generated by another source and interfering with another signal. |
| ITU | International Telecommunication Union. |
| Kbps | Kilobits per second |
| low band | In Australia, mobile network frequencies between 700 MHz to 1 GHz. |
| LTE | Long Term Evolution, the first technical standard to achieve the capabilities requirement for 4G mobile technology known as "IMT Advanced". |
| macro RAN sites | Base station sites that are typically located at a height of 15 to 30 metres above the ground and provide coverage over a large geographic area. They typically carry larger, heavier and higher-powered equipment relative to small cell sites. |
| Massive Machine Type Communications (MMTC) | A capability of 5G which involves enabling mobile networks to manage connectivity for a much larger number of connected devices than was previously possible. |
| Massive MIMO | Massive MIMO refers to MIMO transmission with a much larger number of antenna units, at this stage typically available from vendors with between 16 and 128 antenna units to transmit signals and the same number of antenna units to receive signals. |
| Mbps | Megabits per second. |
| Metro spectrum | Spectrum permitted for use in Adelaide, Brisbane, Canberra, Perth, Melbourne and/or Sydney. |
| mid-band | Mobile network frequencies between 1 GHz to 6 GHz. |
| MIMO | Multiple Input Multiple Output. A radio transmission technique where multiple signals are sent and received from different antennae to created additional data bandwidth. In mobile networks, typically 2–4 antennae are used for MIMO transmission. |
| MNO | Mobile network operator. |
| mobile network | A mobile network uses spectrum and compatible networks to connect mobile devices and deliver mobile services (such as voice, SMS and mobile data) to those devices. It consists of a RAN, a "backhaul" transmission network and a core network. |
| mobile roaming agreements | Agreements between MNOs that allow retail mobile services customers of one MNO to use their mobile device on another MNO’s network when outside the coverage area for the mobile network for which they subscribe. |
| MVNO | Mobile virtual network operator. |
| NSA network | A non-standalone network, which is a type of 5G network that requires an existing 4G network (including the 4G RAN, transmission and core) to provide a 5G service. |
| pole | Power pole, lighting pole, traffic pole or utility pole. |
| radio receivers | Radio receiver is the receiving function, typically receiving a signal from another transmitter, e.g. a device. |
| radio unit | A radio unit includes a Transmitting and Receiving function. May be known as a TRX or RU or Remote RU (RRU). |
| RAN | Radio access networks, consisting of mobile cell towers (also known as base station sites or cell sites), associated electronics and antennae on those sites, that communicate with mobile devices over designated RF spectrum and is connected to the transmission network. |
| refarm or redeploy | The process of redeploying or reusing spectrum for other purposes (for example, redeploying spectrum from 3G to 4G networks). |
| regional spectrum | Spectrum permitted for use in all areas other than Adelaide, Brisbane, Canberra, Perth, Melbourne and Sydney. |
| remote radio unit (RRU) | A Radio Unit that is installed on the structure near to the antenna. |
| RF Spectrum | Radiofrequency (RF) spectrum. Part of the total electromagnetic spectrum which is used for transmitting radio waves. |
| SA network | A standalone network, which is a type of 5G network and core that does not depend on a 4G network to provide 5G services. |
| small cell sites | Base station sites that are typically located at a height of 3 to 10 metres above the ground and provide coverage over a smaller geographic area than Macro RAN sites. They typically carry lighter, smaller and less high-powered equipment than macro RAN sites. |
| SMS | Short message service (also known as text messaging) – a service provided as a feature of the mobile network (as opposed to a messaging service offered by an “over the top” provider like Apple or Facebook). |
| spectrum | See RF Spectrum. |
| spectrum band | A frequency range. |
| spectrum bandwidth | Range of spectrum frequencies, typically within a particular spectrum band. The greater the range of frequencies of spectrum bandwidth the more information can be sent. |
| spectrum licence | Licence from the ACMA to use particular frequencies of spectrum in specific geographic areas as specified in the spectrum licence. |
| spectrum 'lots' | A parcel of spectrum bandwidth that is typically identified as the minimum volume of spectrum that can be purchased in a geographic area at spectrum auction. |
| spectrum propagation | Behaviours and characteristics of radio waves as they travel through a medium e.g. air, buildings, etc. |
| structure | Structures on which equipment deployed on a base station site can be mounted, such as a communications tower or pole, water tower, side of building or rooftop, power pole, lighting pole, traffic pole or utility pole, or other suitable structures. |
| tower | Communications tower or pole, or water tower. |
| transmission | Transmission services supplied over transmission networks. |
| UHF | Ultra-High Frequency, which is a frequency between 300 MHz and 3 GHz. |
| Ultra-Reliable Low Latency Communications | A capability of 5G which means that mission critical systems requiring very low latency and very high reliability such as automated factories and autonomous cars systems can communicate and be managed in near real-time. |
| UMTS | Universal Mobile Telecommunications System |
| VHF | Very High Frequency, which is a frequency between 30 and 300 MHz. |
